  • output: {mode: composite|separations, emulsion: up|down, image: positive|negative, spotsToProcess, inks: [{name, print, frequency, angle}]}: separations print one page per ink in the grey of its coverage (images too; overprinting fills and strokes don't knock out), Registration on every plate; emulsion down mirrors the page and a negative inverts it.
  • graphics: {autoFlatness, flatness, fonts} and color: {intent, preserveNumbers} (the intent colours separate with; without preserveNumbers CMYK colours are separated again).

Halftone screens and a fixed flatness are not written to the PDF (the output device's apply): the warnings say so, as they do for art larger than the imageable area. Type in a font family that isn't available prints in the fallback font, and the warnings of file.print and print.preview name those families. They count only type that prints: visible type that fits its frame or path, on the layers printLayers picks, on the paper of the artboards or tiles that print, and the type in the symbols, patterns, scatter brushes, opacity masks and placed documents of the art that prints. The warnings also give the ids of the area type and type on a path that print and whose text doesn't fit (objects of the document only, not type in the art of symbols, patterns, brushes, opacity masks or placed documents; see Fitting area type).

{"name":"run_command","arguments":{"command":"print.setup","params":{"settings":{"media":"a4","scaling":"fit","marks":{"trim":true}}}}}
{"name":"run_command","arguments":{"command":"print.preview","params":{"settings":{"output":{"mode":"separations"}}}}}
{"name":"run_command","arguments":{"command":"file.print","params":{"path":"/tmp/job.pdf","settings":{"copies":2}}}}

Print to a PostScript file: file.print {format: "postscript"} (the default for a .ps path) writes the same job as PostScript, level: 3 (default) or 2, answering format: "postscript" ("pdf" otherwise; in the app the job is saved, not sent to a printer). The file follows the DSC: %%Pages is the page count (copies included), each %%Page sets its paper size, separations name their ink (%%PlateColor) and set its halftone screen (frequency, angle, a round dot), a fixed flatness is written (setflat), negatives invert the transfer, and the marks print in Registration. PostScript has no transparency: it is flattened first with flattenerPreset (medium by default; high, low or a saved one), with a warning.

{"name":"run_command","arguments":{"command":"file.print","params":{"path":"/tmp/job.ps","level":2,"settings":{"output":{"mode":"separations"}}}}}

Data Recovery

Modified documents get recovery copies (file.recovery.save; the app runs it every autosaveInterval minutes while autosaveRecovery is on, skipping documents of more than 20,000 objects while recoveryOffForComplex is on). A copy goes when its document is saved, reverted or closed, so copies still there after the app quits were left by a crash. The copies live in the recoveryFolder preference's folder (default: Data Recovery beside the app's preferences; none when the app runs with VECTORCRAFT_NO_PREFS) or, on the web, in browser storage.

Each running app (each browser tab) keeps its copies in an area of its own (<area>/<name>): a sub-folder whose .lock file it keeps locked while it runs, or on the web an area with a heartbeat it refreshes every minute and a Web Lock the browser holds until the tab is gone (where the browser has Web Locks: secure pages). Only areas nobody holds are offered: their lock is free, or no tab holds their Web Lock and their heartbeat is older than three intervals (at least three minutes). A background tab whose timers are paused therefore keeps its copies. Without Web Locks another tab can take such a tab for gone; when it resumes, its next heartbeat or recovery save writes its missing copies again. Several apps running at once (agents' instances included) never see each other's copies as crash leftovers, and an area being restored or discarded is held, so two apps launched together never both take it.

file.recovery.list → {copies: [{file, title, path, format, saved, open, running}], location} (open: the copy of a document open here; running: kept by another VectorCraft that is running). file.recovery.restore {file?} opens copies left behind (default: all) as "<name> [Recovered]": modified, and Save asks where to save them (suggesting their original file); the copy moves into this app's area. file.recovery.discard {file?} deletes them. Without a folder or browser storage the commands are disabled and say how to set one.

{"name":"run_command","arguments":{"command":"prefs.set","params":{"key":"recoveryFolder","value":"/tmp/vc-recovery"}}}
{"name":"run_command","arguments":{"command":"file.recovery.save","params":{}}}
{"name":"run_command","arguments":{"command":"file.recovery.restore","params":{"file":"1759650000-1/Poster-1"}}}

Native save options

document.save / file.saveAs / file.saveCopy to a native (.vectorcraft, .vctemplate) or .ai file take, flat or in options (file.formatOptions {format} lists them with their values):

  • separateArtboards: true also writes each artboard of range ("1-3, 5", default "all") to <name>-<artboard>.<ext> beside the file: that artboard and the art touching it. The result's files lists every file written, the master file first (without a path: files: [{name, dataBase64}]). 3 artboards give 4 files.
  • includeLinked: true keeps linked images' own pixels in the file (they stay linked), not just the previews.
  • embedProfiles (default true) carries the ICC profiles the document is tagged with that were loaded from files; opening the file installs them where they are missing (a warning names one that can't be used).
  • pdfCompatible: native files (default false) also carry a PDF of every artboard (pdf in the file); .ai files (default true) with false write blank pages around the native document (smaller; other apps show empty pages).
  • .ai only: compress (default true) is the PDF option compression.compressText.

File → Save As asks for these in the save options dialog (VectorCraft Options) after the save panel, and Save a Copy does too for a .ai file; Save, Save a Copy to a native file and Save as Template reuse the options as last saved (or Use Compression).

{"name":"run_command","arguments":{"command":"file.saveAs","params":{"path":"/tmp/set.vectorcraft","separateArtboards":true,"range":"2-3","includeLinked":true}}}
{"name":"run_command","arguments":{"command":"file.saveAs","params":{"path":"/tmp/set.ai","pdfCompatible":false}}}

EMF and WMF

document.export (and export) with format emf or wmf writes a Windows metafile of the first artboard (or artboard); useArtboards: true writes one file per chosen artboard ({stem}-{artboard}.emf), false the bounds of the visible art. EMF keeps Bézier curves, solid fills (brushes), strokes as geometric pens with their width, caps, joins, miter limit and dashes, clipping masks (path clips), images with their transparency (AlphaBlend) and type as outlines; gradients become images clipped to their shape, pattern fills their tiles clipped to the shape, and raster effects images rendered at the document's raster effects resolution. The header's frame is the artboard in 0.01 mm. WMF flattens curves into polygons in 16-bit units behind a placeable header (1440 units an inch, fewer for pictures over about 22 inches); it has no clipping, transparency or gradients, so clipped art is written whole, images over white and gradients and patterns as one colour. What a format leaves out comes back in warnings. When the picture would hold an image that can't be decoded, the image is left out, and warnings carries "an image couldn't be decoded and is missing from the picture" once for all such images.

document.open and file.place read .emf and .wmf (placeable or not) into one artboard, the picture's frame, and one layer: paths with their fills and strokes (pens become strokes with caps, joins and dashes), clipping groups, images and point type in the font the file names. Records VectorCraft doesn't read (EMF+ drawing among them) are skipped with one warning. clipboard.importEmf {dataBase64, center?} loads an EMF or WMF picture into the clipboard for edit.paste; the app pastes image/emf from the system clipboard ahead of text and bitmaps when the platform's clipboard service offers it (the Windows desktop service doesn't read metafiles yet).

{"name":"export","arguments":{"path":"/tmp/logo.emf"}}
{"name":"run_command","arguments":{"command":"document.export","params":{"format":"wmf","useArtboards":true,"path":"/tmp/icons.wmf"}}}
{"name":"run_command","arguments":{"command":"file.place","params":{"path":"/tmp/chart.emf","at":[300,200]}}}

TIFF, BMP and Targa export

document.export (and export) writes them like the other raster formats: one artboard (or useArtboards, one file each), at ppi, over background, with antiAlias. TIFF takes colorModel (rgb keeps transparency as an unassociated alpha channel, cmyk writes ink amounts in the working CMYK space with CMYK colours keeping their inks, gray is flattened on white), lzw (default true), byteOrder (little, the II files PCs write, or big, MM) and embedIcc (sRGB, the working CMYK profile or grey). BMP takes depth (1 is black and white; 4 and 8 use a palette chosen by reduction and dither; 16, 24, or 32, which keeps transparency), colorModel (rgb or gray), fileFormat (windows or os2: 1, 4, 8 or 24 bits only), rle (RLE4/RLE8 for 4- and 8-bit Windows bitmaps) and flipRows (rows top-down, a negative height; not with rle or os2); impossible combinations are refused. Targa takes depth: 16 (a one-bit alpha), 24 (default) or 32 (with alpha). Formats and depths without alpha are flattened on white. TIFF, BMP and Targa files open again (a 16-bit Targa file with its one-bit alpha); document.formats lists every option.

{"name":"export","arguments":{"path":"/tmp/print.tif","options":{"ppi":300,"colorModel":"cmyk","byteOrder":"big"}}}
{"name":"run_command","arguments":{"command":"document.export","params":{"format":"bmp","depth":8,"rle":true}}}
{"name":"export","arguments":{"path":"/tmp/sprite.tga","options":{"depth":32}}}

Save for Web

File → Export → Save for Web (Legacy) writes optimised web images: document.exportForWeb {format: gif|jpg|png8|png24, …} renders the artboard (clipToArtboard, the default; artboard picks one) or the visible art and the slices, at width, height or percent, and encodes it with the format's settings: for GIF and PNG-8 reduction, colors, dither, ditherAmount, transparency, matte, interlaced, webSnap, lossy (GIF) and colorTable {locked, transparent, webShift, sort}; for JPEG quality, progressive, optimized, embedProfile and matte; for PNG-24 transparency, matte and interlaced. metadata (none, copyright, contact, all) writes File Info as PNG text or a GIF/JPEG comment; convertToSrgb marks PNGs as sRGB. <stem> is the name of path without its extension, or without path the document's title with the characters Windows does not allow in a file name as - and a - after a Windows device name. With slices (slice.list) each image slice becomes images/<slice name>.<ext>, with a - after a Windows device name such as aux (slices: selected writes the selected ones, none one image); output: html adds <stem>.html placing them, with each slice's URL, target, alt text and status message, and No Image / HTML Text cells. Without path the files come back as {name, dataBase64}. As in the raster exports, warnings says when an image couldn't be decoded and is missing from the image; the files are written all the same.

document.exportForWeb.preview returns what Save writes for the same settings without writing it: bytes (the exact file size), seconds to download at kbps (default 56.6), the size, and for palette formats the colour table (colors: [{color, source, transparent, locked, webShifted, webSafe}]). The colour table's lists name colours by source (the colour the reduction made), so a colour keeps its edits when it is web-shifted or snapped. slice: n previews one slice; image: true adds the file; warnings holds the export's warnings.

Presets: webExport.presets.list (built-in ones, then saved ones), webExport.presets.save {name, newName?, …settings}, webExport.presets.delete {name}; any name works as preset in the other commands (its settings, then the keys given). webExport.settings {…} remembers the settings the dialog opens on ({} reads them, reset: true restores the defaults); presets and remembered settings are preferences.

{"name":"run_command","arguments":{"command":"document.exportForWeb.preview","params":{"format":"gif","colors":32,"dither":"none"}}}
{"name":"run_command","arguments":{"command":"document.exportForWeb","params":{"path":"/tmp/web/page.html","format":"png8","output":"html"}}}
{"name":"run_command","arguments":{"command":"webExport.presets.save","params":{"name":"Banner","format":"jpg","quality":70}}}

Asset Export

Assets are pieces of art collected for export on their own (Window › Asset Export, Object › Collect for Export, the Layers panel ≡ menu and row menu, File › Export Selection…). assets.add {ids?, multiple?} collects the selected objects (or ids): one asset per object, or with multiple: false one asset of them all, named after the object or Asset 1, Asset 2…; a layer id collects the art under it (sublayers included). Art already collected keeps its asset. Assets name their objects, so they follow edits: moving the art moves the crop, and deleting it removes it from its asset (an asset left with nothing goes). assets.list gives each asset's id, name, objects and crop (bounds); assets.rename {asset, name} and assets.remove {assets} are undo steps.

assets.export {assets?, folder?, zip?} writes each asset's art alone, cropped to it and named after it, in every format row of the document's export settings, the ones Export for Screens remembers (document.exportSettings); any formats, preset, settings, prefix or subfolders given win for that export. assets.settings.set changes those shared settings (not an undo step). document.exportForScreens {assets: [id…]} does the same with its own params (the dialog's Assets tab). An asset's crop, and the crop of export with selection: true, is the bounds the renderer measures for what the art draws, its effects included.

{"name":"run_command","arguments":{"command":"assets.add","params":{"multiple":true}}}
{"name":"run_command","arguments":{"command":"assets.add","params":{"ids":[12],"multiple":true}}}
{"name":"run_command","arguments":{"command":"assets.settings.set","params":{"formats":[{"format":"png","scale":"1x"},{"format":"png","scale":"2x"},{"format":"svg"}]}}}
{"name":"run_command","arguments":{"command":"assets.export","params":{"zip":true}}}

A layer id in ids (12 above) is the same as Collect for Export on that Layers row: every object under the layer becomes an asset (or one asset with multiple: false).

CSS Properties

css.selection {ids?, units?, position?, dimensions?, unnamed?, rasterize?} returns the CSS web pages style the selected objects (or ids) with, one rule per object, as Window › CSS Properties shows it: a rectangle's or ellipse's fill as background-color or a linear-gradient()/radial-gradient(), its stroke as border, its corners as border-radius; type's font, color, spacing and alignment; opacity, blend mode, shadows and glows (box-shadow, text-shadow) and blur (filter). Layers and plain groups stand for what they hold. Properties SVG export writes too (font, spacing, opacity, blend mode, colours, gradient stops) read exactly as in its style sheets. Each rule in rules says when CSS can't describe the art exactly (unsupported: other shapes, images, patterns, live effects…); it is then written as its box, or with rasterize: true as background-image: url(<class>.png). units is px (1 px per point, as in SVG export), pt, mm, cm or in; unnamed: false leaves out unnamed objects (counted in skipped). css.generate does the same for the whole document, and css.export {path?, scope?: selection|all} writes the .css file with the PNGs of rasterized art next to it (no path: data and images as base64). Its warnings add the PNGs' notes to the CSS's, each once (an image that couldn't be decoded, type written in the fallback font…). In the app, css.copy copies the CSS and css.exportFile writes it through a save panel (the web downloads it) and shows the first note in the status bar.

{"name":"run_command","arguments":{"command":"css.selection","params":{"units":"px","position":true}}}
{"name":"run_command","arguments":{"command":"css.export","params":{"path":"/tmp/site/styles.css","scope":"all","rasterize":true}}}

PSD export

document.export (and export) writes layered bitmaps (.psd, 8 bits per channel) like the other raster formats: one artboard (or useArtboards, one file each), at ppi, with antiAlias, in colorModel rgb, cmyk (ink amounts in the working CMYK space) or gray, with embedIcc. With layers (default true) each top-level layer is a pixel layer of its own, drawn alone and cropped to what it paints, with its name, opacity and blend mode; a background colour becomes a Background layer under them, and the merged image (what readers that skip layers show) is the flat render. maxEditability: true turns layers and sublayers into groups and every object into a layer named as the Layers panel names it (text objects by their text); layers with a clipping mask, an opacity mask, an appearance of their own or knockout stay one pixel layer. Hidden layers and objects are left out unless hiddenLayers: true writes them as hidden layers. layers: false writes one flat image, on white where nothing is drawn. Files are at most 30000 pixels a side.

Photoshop documents (.psd, and .psb, the large document format) open and place as their merged image, the flattened picture every Photoshop file carries (layers aren't read): Bitmap, Grayscale, Duotone, Indexed, RGB, CMYK (converted with the colour settings' CMYK) and Lab, at 1, 8, 16 and 32 bits per channel, sized by their resolution. A document with transparency keeps it. file.place {path} links the file like any image (links.update reads it again when it changes); Multichannel files are refused.

{"name":"export","arguments":{"path":"/tmp/poster.psd","options":{"ppi":300,"maxEditability":true}}}
{"name":"run_command","arguments":{"command":"document.export","params":{"format":"psd","layers":false,"colorModel":"cmyk"}}}

Printing

print.setup {settings?} keeps print settings with the document (one undo step; without settings it answers the current ones): the copies, artboards, paper, orientation, placement, scaling and tiling, marks and bleed, composite or separations output, graphics and colour management options of File › Print. print.preview {settings?} lays the job out without printing: pages, then one copy's sheets, each with its page size, scale, the transform from the document onto the page (pt, y down), the document area it prints and its trim box on the page, plus tiles, the inks of a separation and warnings. file.print {settings?, path?} writes the job as a print-ready PDF (one page per sheet, tile, ink and copy); headless it answers dataBase64. In the app, file.print with no params opens the Print dialog, and with params it prints through the system (printer: a name from print.printers, default the system's default printer) or, with toFile, a path or no printing available, saves the PDF.

{"name":"run_command","arguments":{"command":"print.setup","params":{"settings":{"media":"a4","scaling":"fit","marks":{"trim":true}}}}}
{"name":"run_command","arguments":{"command":"print.preview","params":{"settings":{"copies":2,"output":{"mode":"separations"}}}}}
{"name":"run_command","arguments":{"command":"file.print","params":{"path":"/tmp/job.pdf"}}}

Print presets

print.presets.list lists [Default] (the default print settings, builtIn: true, protected) and the saved presets, each with its settings and how they differ from [Default] (changed). print.presets.save {name?, newName?, preset?, settings?} creates or changes a saved preset (starting from its own settings, or from preset, with settings over them) and print.presets.delete {name} deletes one; [Default] can be neither changed nor deleted. print.presets.export {names?, path?} writes them as a .vcprintpresets JSON file (without path it returns data), and print.presets.import {path? | data? | dataBase64?, replace?} adds a file's presets (a name in use gets a number unless replace). Saved presets live with the preferences. print.setup, print.preview and file.print take preset, the name of [Default] or of a saved preset in any case: the preset's settings are used instead of the document's, with settings over them, and print.setup {preset} saves them with the document. An unknown name is refused with the list of presets.

{"name":"run_command","arguments":{"command":"print.presets.save","params":{"name":"Posters","settings":{"scaling":"tileImageable","overlap":18,"marks":{"trim":true}}}}}
{"name":"run_command","arguments":{"command":"file.print","params":{"preset":"Posters","path":"/tmp/posters.pdf"}}}

EPS and PostScript import

document.open and file.place read .eps, .epsf and .ps files, and PostScript by any name (an .ai saved without PDF compatibility opens as artwork; an .ait one as a new untitled document). A .ps file reports the format ps, which document.export doesn't write (file.print writes PostScript). An EPS file VectorCraft wrote restores the document it carries (restored: true, as Preserve Editing does for PDF and SVG); when that document can't be read the PostScript is read instead and the first warning says why.

Other files are run through a small PostScript interpreter (Level 3, first page only): paths, fills and strokes with their width, caps, joins, miter limit and dashes (a fill and a stroke of the same path become one object), grey, RGB, CMYK, indexed and spot colours (a Separation ink becomes a spot swatch, painted at its tint), clips (clipping groups; clipsave/cliprestore), gsave/grestore, save/restore, transforms, procedures with bind def, loops, dictionaries (the standard ones are values in systemdict; dictstack, internaldict), arrays and strings whose intervals share storage, resources (categories made from Generic, resourceforall), executable filters (cvx exec runs their data; flushfile skips it), axial and radial shadings and shading patterns (gradients), images and image masks (data in the file through ASCII85, hex, run-length, Flate, LZW or DCT filters, or from procedures), and type as point type in the font the file names (embedded font programs are skipped). In a file in Illustrator's own format (Illustrator 3–8 .ai and their EPS, written with the prolog that defines its operators) the groups it writes (u … U) come in as groups, nested as they were; clips and groups nest at most 128 deep. The artboard is the %%HiResBoundingBox (else %%BoundingBox; a letter page without one). A program the interpreter can't run (an operator it doesn't know, an error, a runaway loop) or that draws nothing comes in as its TIFF preview (palette previews with an alpha channel too) with a warning; without a preview, the art drawn up to the error is kept with a warning, and a file with none is refused with a message saying why. A PostScript error is named as PostScript spells it, followed by what it means: a PostScript error (typecheck in `add`; a value has the wrong type for the operator). The status bar shows the meaning in the interface language, and the name stays as it is.

{"name":"run_command","arguments":{"command":"document.open","params":{"path":"/tmp/logo.eps"}}}
{"name":"run_command","arguments":{"command":"file.place","params":{"path":"/tmp/logo.eps","at":[300,300]}}}

PDF/X

document.exportPdf {standard: "pdfX1a" | "pdfX3" | "pdfX4"} (or the built-in PDF/X presets) writes print exchange files as their standards say. Every PDF/X file gets a /GTS_PDFX output intent (output.outputIntent; blank, the CMYK profile in effect: the destination when converting to CMYK, else the working one, embedded; in PDF/X-1a and PDF/X-3 a name that isn't a profile needs output.registry, a registered printing condition named without a profile; PDF/X-4 always embeds one), a TrimBox on every page (the artboard), /Trapped True or False, /GTS_PDFXVersion, a title (Untitled when the document has none) and dates; type is outlined or embedded; editing data, passwords and, in PDF/X-1a and PDF/X-3, PDF layers are refused.

  • pdfX1a (PDF/X-1a:2001, written as PDF 1.3): CMYK, grey and spot colours only. Every colour and image is converted to output.destination (blank: the output intent's CMYK profile, else the working CMYK profile), untagged; an RGB destination or output intent is refused. Transparency is flattened before writing.
  • pdfX3 (PDF/X-3:2002, written as PDF 1.3): colours are tagged with ICC profiles; transparency is flattened.
  • pdfX4 (PDF/X-4:2010, PDF 1.6 at most): transparency and layers are kept, colours are tagged, and the XMP metadata names the standard.

Flattening uses flattenerPreset (default High Resolution, see PDF 1.3 above) on a copy (the document is untouched; a warning says so), and images with see-through pixels count as transparency. The written file is checked against its standard: what it still breaks (transparency, RGB in PDF/X-1a, a font not embedded) fails the export instead of writing a file that only claims the standard.

{"name":"run_command","arguments":{"command":"document.exportPdf","params":{"path":"/tmp/press.pdf","preset":"PDF/X-1a:2001","output":{"outputIntent":"VectorCraft Generic CMYK (SWOP-like)","outputCondition":"Coated","trapped":false}}}}

Print tiling

View → Show Print Tiling (view.printTiling {on?}, per document, not undoable) draws the pages of the document's print settings on the canvas: each page's paper edge, its imageable area dashed, numbered (tiles across then down; tiles outside tileRange dimmer). The Print Tiling tool (printTiling) shows them too. print.tiling {settings?} answers what is drawn, from the layout print.preview and both writers (PDF and PostScript) use, so the canvas and the printed job agree: {tileOrigin, pages: [{artboard, number, printed, page, imageable}]} ([x0, y0, x1, y1] in document space; every tile when tiling, else each page once, not once per ink).

Dragging with the tool puts the top-left corner of the first page's imageable area where the pointer is (snapping to the artboard's edges): print.tiling.set {origin: [x, y], artboard?} saves it with the print settings as tileOrigin: {placed: true, x, y}, measured from the artboard's top-left corner (one undo step). It replaces the placement, and tiles start there (going on left and up as far as the art does). A double click on the canvas, or on the tool's button, runs print.tiling.set {reset: true}: the placement places the pages again. In the Print dialog the placement then shows as placed by the tool, with a Reset button; dragging the preview moves the origin.

{"name":"run_command","arguments":{"command":"view.printTiling","params":{"on":true}}}
{"name":"run_command","arguments":{"command":"print.tiling.set","params":{"origin":[-36,-36]}}}
{"name":"run_command","arguments":{"command":"print.tiling","params":{"settings":{"scaling":"tileImageable","scale":{"width":300,"height":300}}}}}

Print advanced options

The Print dialog's Advanced section and the printer profile are print settings too (print.setup, print.preview, file.print): advanced: {printAsBitmap, overprints: preserve|discard|simulate, flattenerPreset} and color: {profile}. In composite output, preserve writes overprinting fills and strokes with an overprinting graphics state (/OP, as PDF export does), discard makes overprinting fills and strokes knock out and simulate prints them as Overprint Preview shows them (darkened); either way the file has no overprint left. Separations always keep overprints. flattenerPreset (High Resolution, Medium Resolution, Low Resolution or a saved preset, flattener.presets.list) flattens transparency before printing, as Object → Flatten Transparency does; empty, a PDF keeps it live (PostScript, which has none, uses file.print's flattenerPreset, else this one, else medium). printAsBitmap prints each composite page as one image of the art at the document's raster effects resolution. color.profile names an RGB or CMYK profile: composite PDF colours are converted to it with color.intent (CMYK colours keep their numbers with preserveNumbers), so a CMYK profile writes device CMYK; separations separate with a CMYK one. Settings saved before these options load with their defaults.

{"name":"run_command","arguments":{"command":"file.print","params":{"path":"/tmp/proof.pdf","settings":{"advanced":{"overprints":"simulate","flattenerPreset":"High Resolution"},"color":{"profile":"VectorCraft Generic CMYK (SWOP-like)","intent":"perceptual"}}}}}

Rotated bounding boxes

Every object keeps the angle of its own axes (counter-clockwise degrees; document.inspect reports it as a node's rotation, and the selection's shared angle as selectionRotation, 0 when the selected objects differ). Transforms turn it with the object while its path geometry is still baked, so after a rotation the bounding box and its handles stay square to it, as in the reference app. object.rotate {angle, absolute: true} turns the selection to an angle (the Transform and Properties panels' Rotate field), and object.setBounds measures width, height and the reference point along the turned box (x, y stay page coordinates). Objects turned together share the box. A new group starts square to the page. object.resetBoundingBox squares the box again without moving the art.

{"name":"run_command","arguments":{"command":"object.rotate","params":{"angle":45,"absolute":true}}}

Select All while editing type

While the Type tool edits text (a click into type, pointer_gesture with "tool":"type"), select.all (Cmd+A, press_key {key: "A", mods: {cmd: true}}) selects all of that text instead of the art, as in the reference app, and returns {editing, start, end} (the text's id and the selected byte range, which text.getRange and text.setRangeStyle take); the art selection stays as it is. Without text being edited it selects every object and returns {count}. Text in threaded frames is selected one frame at a time.

{"name":"press_key","arguments":{"key":"A","mods":{"cmd":true}}}

Empty point type

Point type the Type tool places with a click and leaves empty is discarded when editing ends: Escape, another tool, a click elsewhere, switching documents, or a command that takes the text out of the selection (select.none, a select.set of other objects). The steps since the click go with it (text.discardEmpty {id}), so the document and its undo history are as if the click never happened. Commands on the edited text (the Character panel's text.setRangeStyle {id}) keep it in editing. Area type dragged as a frame and type in or on a path keep their frame (Object → Path → Clean Up removes empty text).

{"name":"run_command","arguments":{"command":"text.discardEmpty","params":{"id":42}}}

Resizing area type

As in the reference app, dragging a bounding-box handle of area type resizes its type area instead of scaling the type: the Selection tool sends object.transform {matrix, typeAreas: true}, which reshapes the frame of each area type object it transforms (other objects, type inside groups and point type transform as usual) and reflows the text at its size, through its thread too. text.reshapeArea {id, anchors: [[subpath, anchor]…], dx, dy} moves frame anchors with their handles (Direct Selection dragging a corner, or an edge's two ends), and text.areaOptions {width?, height?} sizes the area from its top-left corner (the query returns both). Each is one undo step. Object › Transform › Scale, the Scale tool and the Transform panel still scale the type. text.reshapePath {id, anchors: [[subpath, anchor]…], dx, dy} does the same for the path of type on a path (Direct Selection dragging one of its anchors): the start and end brackets keep their fractions of the path and the text reflows along it.

{"name":"run_command","arguments":{"command":"text.reshapeArea","params":{"id":42,"anchors":[[0,2]],"dx":40,"dy":60}}}

Vertical alignment

text.areaOptions {verticalAlign: top|center|bottom|justify} (Area Type Options › Align) places the lines of each row/column cell on its own: center centres the block of lines in the cell, bottom puts the last line's descent on the cell's bottom (inside the inset), justify keeps the first line at the top, moves the last one to the bottom and shares the space left over equally between the lines (there is no paragraph spacing limit: every line gap grows by the same amount). A cell with a single line justifies to the top, and a full or overflowing cell only moves by the less-than-a-line of space it has left. In rectangular frames without text wrap the lines just move; in other frames (and around wrap objects) the text flows again starting lower until it settles, never losing text that fit top-aligned. Vertical type aligns along its block axis (bottom is the frame's left edge). Exports lay type out again, so SVG, PDF and EPS show the aligned text.

{"name":"run_command","arguments":{"command":"text.areaOptions","params":{"verticalAlign":"center"}}}

Fitting area type: Auto Size, Shrink Text to Fit, overflow

text.areaOptions (and text.create's area, text.createInPath in area mode) take a fit:

  • none (default): the frame keeps its size; text that doesn't fit overflows (the red "+").
  • autoHeight: Auto Size. After every edit (typing, text.editRange, character and paragraph changes, a new width) the frame's bottom moves to just below the last line plus the inset, in the same undo step as the edit. In several columns the frame gets the least height (to 0.01 pt) at which the columns hold the text. It applies to rectangular frames of horizontal type in one row; height is ignored while it is on, and setting the height by hand (a handle drag, text.reshapeArea moving the bottom) turns it off, as does threading the frame. The autoSizeAreaType preference ("Auto Size New Area Type") gives new area type autoHeight.
  • shrinkText (beyond the reference app), with fitMinPercent (10–100, default 50): when the text overflows, every run's size, leading (explicit leading; auto leading follows the size) and baseline shift are scaled by the largest factor down to fitMinPercent % that makes it fit. Paragraph spacing and indents stay. The stored sizes don't change: the scaling happens at layout time, so rendering and every export see it. The factor is found by bisection over the first run's size in steps of 0.1 pt (its scaled size is a whole number of tenths of a point, so the result is deterministic); at fitMinPercent the text may still overflow.

The file stores "fit": "autoHeight" or "fit": {"shrinkText": {"minPercent": 40}} in the object's area; the command also accepts that object form. The query (and every reply) of text.areaOptions reports fit as its id, fitMinPercent, overflow (the text doesn't fit its frame) and fitScale (Shrink Text's factor, 1 unshrunk). document.inspect (and document.node {summary: true}) reports overflow for area type and type on a path (text past the end of the path), and fit and fitScale for area type.

Text that doesn't fit is drawn up to the last line that does, so a one-line headline in a frame lower than its line draws nothing. The commands that make type or change what it holds say so in their own reply: text.create, text.createInPath, text.setText and text.setStyle add overflow: [id…] (the area type and type on a path among the objects they made or changed whose text doesn't fit), and the add_text tool adds overflow: true. The key is left out when everything fits. text.create and text.createInPath also return bounds: {x, y, width, height}, the new type's box as document.inspect reports it (after justify), so a script can place the type without a helper object (#2413). The warnings of file.print and print.preview give the ids of the objects that print and whose text doesn't fit: the first 20 ids, then how many more.

In PDF, EPS, SVG, EMF, WMF and the raster formats, the warnings of document.export, document.exportPdf and document.exportEps give the ids in the same way for the visible objects that the export draws and whose text doesn't fit. The export draws the objects on the pages or artboards it writes; an EPS of the visible art and an SVG without chosen artboards draw all of them. A PDF leaves out the layers that don't print unless includeNonPrinting or createLayers is set. A .ai file keeps the whole text in its editing data and gives no such warning.

{"name":"run_command","arguments":{"command":"text.areaOptions","params":{"fit":"shrinkText","fitMinPercent":60}}}

Threading text from the in and out ports

As in the reference app, selected area type and type on a path show an in port and an out port: area type's on the frame's left side just below the top-left corner and on its right side just above the bottom-right corner (so the corner handles stay free), type on a path's just before its start bracket and just past its end bracket. A port is empty while the frame isn't threaded on that side, shows an arrow when it is, and the out port shows a red "+" when the text doesn't fit (with more than 1,000 such objects selected no ports are drawn). A click with the Selection tool on the out port loads the pointer with the text. The next click threads it into the closed path, area type or type on a path under the pointer, which becomes the next frame; a click on empty canvas makes a new frame of the first one's kind, shape and size with its top-left corner at the click, and a drag draws a rectangle for the new frame. A click on the in port works the same way, but the frame placed comes before the clicked one: the story starts there and flows on into it. Escape or another tool drops the loaded text. Each placement is one undo step (text.thread.add), which agents can run directly:

text.thread.add {from, to? | rect?: [x, y, width, height] | at?: [x, y], before?} threads area type or type on a path from into one more frame, put right after it in its thread (a new thread of the two when from has none), or right before it with before: true (the in port): to, a closed path (converted to an empty frame), area type or type on a path that is not threaded yet; rect, a new rectangular frame (positive width and height); or at, a new frame of from's kind, shape and size whose top-left is at at. Give exactly one of the three. New frames take from's type options, paint and first character and paragraph attributes, and go just above it. The story reflows through the thread, the two frames are selected, and it returns {id}, the frame threaded into. It refuses an id that doesn't exist, a from that is point type, a to that is an open path, from itself, inside a compound path or already in a thread (from may be threaded already: the frame goes in beside it), coordinates that aren't finite numbers and a before that isn't true or false.

A double-click on a port where the frame is threaded breaks the thread there (one undo step, text.thread.break):

text.thread.break {id, port: "in" | "out"} splits the thread of area type or type on a path id before it (in) or after it (out). The whole story flows into the frames before the break; the frames after it are left empty, still threaded with each other when two or more remain, and the first of them starts a paragraph. It refuses an id that doesn't exist, a port other than in or out, and a side where no frame of a thread comes before or after id.

In every thread, a paragraph that continues from one frame into the next has its first line, first-line indent and bullet or number in the frame where it starts. In the next frame its first line starts where its wrapped lines start, and it has no bullet or number. In a frame that is no longer threaded (after text.thread.releaseSelection or text.thread.remove), in a copy of a frame and in point type converted from one, the first paragraph has its first-line indent and bullet or number again.

{"name":"run_command","arguments":{"command":"text.thread.add","params":{"from":42,"at":[300,120]}}}

type.createOutlines (Type › Create Outlines) and effect.expandAppearance (when it outlines type, as Expand Appearance describes) take an outlined frame of threaded text out of its thread with the text it shows, and the other frames keep the text they hold. When the thread's last frame is outlined, the text it has no room for, which its outlines do not draw, goes after the text of the last frame that stays. A thread left with one frame is no longer a thread. When effect.expandAppearance keeps a frame as type in a group (type with no fills or strokes of its own whose only effects are shadows, outer glows or Crop Marks), the type stays in the thread in the frame's place. Object › Expand (object.expand) outlines type with these two commands. object.flattenTransparency takes each frame of threaded text that it outlines (textToOutlines) or turns into flat regions or an image out of its thread in the same way. symbol.new (New Symbol) and symbol.update (Redefine Symbol) take each frame of threaded text in the selection out of its thread in the same way, and the symbol's art holds a copy of the frame with the text it shows. When every frame of a thread goes into the symbol, the copy of the last frame keeps the text it has no room for.

Converting between point type and area type

With the Selection tool, a single selected point or area type object shows the type widget: a small circle beside the middle of its bounding box's right side, hollow on point type and filled on area type. Double-clicking it runs type.convertToAreaType (point type gets a frame around its text, which keeps its place and doesn't rewrap) or type.convertToPointType (soft line wraps become line breaks), the same commands as the Type menu's Convert To Area Type and Convert To Point Type. Either keeps the text and its styles and is one undo step; ids picks the objects (default: the selection). A frame of threaded text that type.convertToPointType converts leaves its thread with the text it holds, and the story flows through the other frames without that text; a thread left with one frame is no longer a thread.

{"name":"run_command","arguments":{"command":"type.convertToAreaType","params":{"ids":[42]}}}

Hyphenation options

text.setFormat sets the Paragraph panel menu's Hyphenation… options of the selected type (or ids, or the paragraphs a start / end range touches), which apply while hyphenate is on:

  • wordsLongerThan (letters, 1 to 25, default 5): only words with more letters than this are hyphenated.
  • afterFirst and beforeLast (letters, 1 to 15, default 3): the fewest letters a word keeps before and after the hyphen.
  • hyphenLimit (lines, 0 to 25, default 0): the most consecutive lines that may end with a hyphen; 0 sets no limit. A line that ends at a soft hyphen counts, and a soft hyphen still breaks a line past the limit.
  • hyphenationZone (pt, 0 to 1296, default 0): with the Single-line Composer, a line whose text before the next word already ends within this distance of the right edge breaks before that word instead of hyphenating it. The Every-line Composer does not use it.
  • hyphenateCapitalized (default true): false leaves words that start with a capital letter whole. Words in capitals only are never hyphenated.

The letter and line counts are rounded, and every value is clamped to its range. Preferences › Hyphenation › Exceptions still decide the breaks of a listed word. The options are saved with the paragraph (hyphenation in para) only when they differ from the defaults, and paragraph styles carry them (paraStyle.new {attrs: {hyphenation: {words_longer_than: 8}}}). One undo step. ui.hyphenation opens the Hyphenation dialog on the values the Paragraph panel shows; OK sends the values that changed.

{"name":"run_command","arguments":{"command":"text.setFormat","params":{"hyphenate":true,"wordsLongerThan":8,"hyphenLimit":2}}}

Justification options

text.setFormat sets the Paragraph panel menu's Justification… options of the selected type (or ids, or the paragraphs a start / end range touches), in percent:

  • wordSpacing (0 to 1000, default 80 / 100 / 133), letterSpacing (-100 to 500, a share of the font's word space, default 0 / 0 / 0) and glyphScaling (50 to 200, default 100 / 100 / 100), each [min, desired, max] or {min?, desired?, max?} (the values not given are kept). Desired applies to every line: word spaces are that share of the font's word space, each letter followed by another is followed by that share of a word space, and glyphs are that wide. A justified line may move them between Minimum and Maximum to fill its measure: word spacing first, then letter spacing, then glyph scaling; past all three its word spaces take the rest. Values are clamped to their range, and Minimum ≤ Desired ≤ Maximum must hold in every paragraph afterwards, or nothing changes and the command fails.
  • autoLeading (0 to 500, default 120): the leading of characters whose leading is Auto, as a share of their size.
  • singleWordJustification ("fullJustify" (default), "alignLeft", "alignCenter" or "alignRight"): how a justified line holding a single word is set.

The options are saved with the paragraph (justification in para) only when they differ from the defaults, and paragraph styles carry them (paraStyle.new {attrs: {justification: {auto_leading: 150}}}). Vertical type isn't glyph scaled, and type on a path doesn't use them. One undo step. ui.justification opens the Justification dialog on the values the Paragraph panel shows, with Preview; OK sends the values that changed.

{"name":"run_command","arguments":{"command":"text.setFormat","params":{"wordSpacing":[80,100,150],"letterSpacing":{"max":5},"singleWordJustification":"alignLeft"}}}

Insert Kashidas

text.setFormat {kashida} (Paragraph panel › Insert Kashidas, shown with the Indic options) sets how the justified lines of the paragraphs of the selected type (or ids, or the paragraphs a start / end range touches) lengthen their Arabic words: "none" (the default), "short", "medium", "long" or "stylistic". On a justified line of horizontal area type that is shorter than its measure, each Arabic word with joined letters gets one kashida between its last pair of them: a tatweel (U+0640) in the font of the letter after it. Words end at spaces, inline graphics and characters other than letters and marks, such as punctuation, digits and symbols. The kashidas share the line's room equally, each at most a quarter of an em (short), half an em (medium) or an em (long) of that letter's size at its horizontal scale; a stylistic kashida is a row of the font's own tatweels, as many whole ones as fit in its share and in an em. The word spaces and the rest of justification take the room the kashidas leave, so the line keeps its width. Line breaks are the same as with none. Lines that are not justified, words without joined letters and fonts without a tatweel get no kashidas. Each tatweel is a glyph of the layout that stands for no character and does not advance; the letter after the kashida advances over it, so the caret before that letter, a selection of it and a click on it take the kashida in. The setting is saved with the paragraph (kashida in para) only when it is not none, paragraph styles carry it, and Reset Panel sets it back to none. The change is one undo step.

{"name":"run_command","arguments":{"command":"text.setFormat","params":{"kashida":"medium"}}}

Kinsoku Set

text.setFormat {kinsoku: "hard"|"soft"|"none"} sets the Paragraph panel's Kinsoku Set of the selected type (or ids): which Japanese characters may not start or end a line. Hard (the default; new type and documents from before the setting) keeps closing brackets, commas, full stops, middle dots, iteration marks, the prolonged sound mark ー and small kana off the line start, and opening brackets off the line end. Soft lets 々, ー and small kana start a line, as JLREQ's level 3 line-breaking rules do (https://www.w3.org/TR/jlreq/#addendum_a3); the rest of the set stays. None applies no kinsoku. hard isn't saved. One undo step.

{"name":"run_command","arguments":{"command":"text.setFormat","params":{"kinsoku":"soft"}}}

Hanging punctuation (burasagari)

text.setFormat {burasagari: "none"|"standard"|"forced"} sets the Paragraph panel menu's Burasagari (None / Regular / Force) of the selected type (or ids): an East Asian comma or full stop ending an area type line (、。，．, half-width ､｡) hangs outside the frame, Regular only when it doesn't fit, Force always (the rest of the line then fills the measure). Closing brackets and Latin punctuation don't hang; point type, right-to-left and hyphenated lines are left as they are. The hanging mark is as wide as its punctuation spacing makes it (mojikumi). New type (the Type tools, text.create) takes standard; documents from before it and imported text keep none, and none isn't saved. One undo step.

{"name":"run_command","arguments":{"command":"text.setFormat","params":{"burasagari":"forced"}}}

Optical Margin Alignment

type.opticalMarginAlignment {on?: bool} (Type › Optical Margin Alignment) turns Optical Margin Alignment on or off for the selected type objects (or ids); without on it sets the opposite of the first object's setting. It replies {on, ids}, and the menu item is checked while the first type object it acts on (inside a selected group too) has it on. Optical Margin Alignment is a setting of the whole thread: the command sets every frame of a selected frame's thread, and ids lists them all. Threading frames gives the thread its first frame's setting, frames taken out of a thread keep it, and a file whose frames of one thread differ opens with the first frame's setting in all of them. In every paragraph of point and area type, these characters hang outside the margin when they start or end a line: quotation marks, hyphens (a hyphenated line's hyphen too), full stops and commas hang wholly; asterisks, tildes, ellipses, en and em dashes, colons and semicolons hang by half their width; and A, V, W, Y, v, w and y hang by a tenth of theirs. A punctuation mark followed by a quotation mark hangs with it. Left-aligned lines hang at their left margin, right-aligned lines hang at their right margin, and centered and justified lines hang at both. Auto aligns a right-to-left line at its right. A right-to-left line is read from right to left: where it hangs at its left margin, ." ending it hangs both glyphs. In vertical type the margins are the top and bottom of a column. The space that justification adds between glyphs leaves out the gaps next to a hanging punctuation mark. Lines break where they did without it, and a line of one glyph does not hang. East Asian commas and full stops hang only through burasagari, and a line that ends in one hangs only at its start. Type on a path hangs at its brackets, measured along the path: left-aligned, Justify All and Justify with Last Line Aligned Left type hangs at the start bracket, right-aligned and Justify with Last Line Aligned Right type hangs at the end bracket, and centered and Justify with Last Line Aligned Center type hangs at both. Auto type hangs at the bracket it is placed at: the end bracket for horizontal type whose first paragraph runs right to left, and the start bracket otherwise. Glyphs that hang past the end bracket do not make the type overflow. On an open path a hang stops at the path's end, and on a closed path the two hangs together take at most the stretch between the end and start brackets. SVG export writes type on a path whose glyphs hang as outlines, with a warning. New type and documents from before it have it off, and it is saved only when on. One undo step.

{"name":"run_command","arguments":{"command":"type.opticalMarginAlignment","params":{"on":true}}}

Roman Hanging Punctuation

text.setFormat {romanHangingPunctuation: bool} (Paragraph panel menu › Roman Hanging Punctuation) turns it on or off for the paragraphs of the selected type (or ids, or the paragraphs a start / end range touches). In those paragraphs the punctuation Optical Margin Alignment hangs hangs outside the margin by the same amounts (quotation marks, hyphens, full stops and commas wholly; asterisks, tildes, ellipses, en and em dashes, colons and semicolons by half), on the same sides (left-aligned lines at their left, right-aligned at their right, centered and justified at both), and letters don't hang. With Optical Margin Alignment on as well, the letters hang too. East Asian punctuation hangs only through burasagari. When the first paragraph of type on a path has Roman Hanging Punctuation on, the type hangs its punctuation at its brackets as Optical Margin Alignment does, and no letters. Off by default, saved with the paragraph (roman_hanging in para) only when on, carried by paragraph styles, and turned off by Reset Panel. One undo step. The menu item is checked while the paragraph the Paragraph panel shows has it on.

{"name":"run_command","arguments":{"command":"text.setFormat","params":{"romanHangingPunctuation":true}}}

Bulleted and numbered lists

text.setFormat {list: "none"|"bullet"|"number", bullet: "none"|"dot"|"dash"|"circle"|"square", number: "none"|"arabic"|"upperLetters"|"lowerLetters"|"upperRoman"|"lowerRoman"|"twoDigits"|"threeDigits"} (Paragraph panel › Bullets and Numbered List) makes the paragraphs of the selected type (or ids, or the paragraphs a start / end range touches) a bulleted or numbered list or takes them out of one. bullet sets the bullet: a filled dot (•), a dash (–), a hollow circle (◦) or a filled square (▪). list: "bullet" keeps the bullet of a paragraph that is already in a list and gives the others the dot. number sets the number style: 1, 2, 3; A, B, C; a, b, c; I, II, III; i, ii, iii; 01, 02, 03; or 001, 002, 003. list: "number" keeps the style of a paragraph that is already in a numbered list and gives the others 1, 2, 3. Without list, a number style makes a numbered list and a bullet makes a bulleted one; a numbered list keeps its bullet for when it is made bulleted again. "none" in any of the three keys turns the list off and leaves the indents as they are. An unknown value is an error and changes nothing. A paragraph that joins a list while its left and first-line indents are both 0 gets a left indent of 18 pt and a first-line indent of -18 pt, unless the same call sets leftIndent or firstLineIndent; other indents stay as they are, also when a list changes between bullets and numbers. In a horizontal right-to-left list paragraph the indents are mirrored: the left and first-line indents are measured from the right edge, and the right indent from the left edge. Its marker starts the first line at the right, and the lines it wraps to start at the right where the first line's text starts after the marker. A right-to-left paragraph without a list keeps its left and first-line indents at the left, and vertical type keeps its indents as they are. In threaded type, a list paragraph that continues into the next frame starts that frame where its wrapped lines start, without the bullet or number, and the paragraph after it takes the next number.

The layout draws the marker (the bullet, or the number and its period) before the first line of each list paragraph that has text, in the font, size, scales and color of the paragraph's first character, without that character's baseline shift, rotation, superscript or subscript, tracking, manual kerning and tate-chu-yoko. The marker is not a character of the text: copied text leaves it out, the caret before the paragraph's first character stands at the text, a selection does not highlight the marker, and Touch Type moves that character without the marker. The text after it starts at the next tab stop, the left indent counting as a stop when no explicit stop comes before it, measured from the right in a right-to-left list paragraph; a number that reaches past the left indent takes the text to the next tab stop after it. The lines the paragraph wraps to start at the left indent. Type on a path draws the marker and the same gap before the paragraph's text; a right-to-left item on a path has its marker at the right of its text. Justification and Optical Margin Alignment leave the marker and the gap after it as they are, and an underline or strikethrough starts at the text. Return at the end of a list item starts another item in the same list. The list is saved with the paragraph (list in para, for example {"bullet": "square"} or {"bullet": "dot", "number": "upperRoman"}) only when it is set, and paragraph styles carry it: a style captured from a paragraph without a list turns the list off. Reset Panel turns it off. The change is one undo step.

A numbered paragraph's number counts the numbered paragraphs of its story up to and including it: the count runs through consecutive list paragraphs, through the frames of threaded type in thread order, and starts again at 1 after a paragraph in no list. An empty paragraph has no number and is not counted, and a bulleted paragraph keeps its bullet and is not counted. Letters run from A to Z, then AA to ZZ, then AAA; roman numerals use IV, IX, XL, XC, CD and CM; 01 and 001 keep every digit past 99 and 999; letters and roman numerals past 3999 are written in Arabic digits. In a right-to-left paragraph the number reads left to right with its period at its left. Each threaded frame saves the count that its earlier frames carry into it (listCount, only when it is not 0). Type that is in no thread counts from 1: after Remove Threading or Release Selection, in a frame converted to point type, and in every copy of a frame (pasted, duplicated, copied in the Layers panel, or taken out of a symbol or a placed file). A symbol made of a thread keeps the numbers in the symbol's art.

The canvas, EPS, PDF and Type › Create Outlines draw the marker. PDF writes the marker as text of its own, where the canvas draws it, and a PDF viewer selects, copies and finds its characters. Each of its glyphs stands for the marker character that the glyph's font maps to that glyph in its character map. Glyphs that an OpenType feature such as oldstyle figures replaced stand for the characters left over, in order, when there are as many of those glyphs as characters left over. A marker glyph that stands for no character, such as the missing-glyph box when no font has a glyph for it, is drawn as a path. Live SVG text writes the marker's characters before the paragraph's text, except type on a path with a list, which it writes as outlines with a warning. DXF writes the marker as a TEXT entity of its own, its characters in the order they are drawn. Legacy .ai writes the paragraph's text without the marker and warns that bulleted and numbered lists are left out.

Type › Bullets and Numbering › Apply Bullets and the canvas context menu's Bullets and Numbering › Apply Bullets run type.applyBullets {bullet?} on the selected type's paragraphs, or on those the Type tool's selection touches. Without bullet it runs text.setFormat {list: "bullet"}, and with it it runs text.setFormat {bullet}. Apply Numbers in the same menus runs type.applyNumbers {number?}. Without number it runs text.setFormat {list: "number"}, and with it it runs text.setFormat {number}.

Type › Bullets and Numbering › Convert to Text runs type.convertListToText on the selected type's paragraphs, or on those the Type tool's selection touches, and that runs text.convertListToText {ids?, start?, end?}. The marker of each list paragraph becomes characters at the start of the paragraph: the bullet, or the number and its period as the paragraph showed it, then a tab. They are set in the marker's font, size and color: the style of the paragraph's first character without the attributes the marker leaves out. The paragraph leaves its list. When the marker's tab went to the left indent, the paragraph gets a left-aligned tab stop without a leader there, in place of any stop there, so that the text after the tab starts where it started after the marker. When the marker's tab went to a tab stop, the converted tab goes to the same place when that stop is left-aligned and no stop there or past it has a leader. When a stop there or past it has a leader or is not left-aligned, or a right, center or decimal stop lies between the marker and the place its tab ended, the paragraph gets a left-aligned stop without a leader where the marker's tab ended, in place of any stop there and of those right, center and decimal stops before it. A paragraph that holds a tab of its own keeps its stops past the left indent as they are. The characters are text like any other: they can be selected, styled, edited and deleted, and copied text includes them. In threaded type the numbers are the ones the story counted through its frames, and a paragraph that continues into the next frame gets its characters once, where its text starts. An empty list paragraph has no marker and only leaves the list. A numbered paragraph after the converted ones that stays in its list counts from 1 again, as after any paragraph in no list. A paragraph with no direction set runs the way its first strong character does; when a letter or a roman numeral put before right-to-left text would make it run left to right, the paragraph gets the Right to Left direction. A right-to-left paragraph that leaves its list no longer has its indents mirrored, and on a path the tab takes its own width, as every tab on a path does. With the Every-line Composer, a paragraph that holds a tab is broken line by line, so a converted paragraph of several lines can break at other places than its list paragraph did. The change is one undo step, and the reply gives the ids of the type it changed, how many paragraphs were converted, and the characters inserted, each with the type object's id, the byte at which they went in (counted in the text before the change) and their length in bytes. While the Type tool edits, text.convertListToText ends its typing first and moves its anchor and caret past the characters inserted at or before them, whether the call comes from the menu, MCP or the control channel. In threaded type, when the caret's character moved to another frame, the Type tool edits that frame with the caret next to the same character, and that frame becomes the selection when it was not selected.

Typing starts a list too. When the Type tool types a space on its own (one key press, or type_text {text: " "}) right after 1. or 1), A. or A), or one of *, #, > and + at the start of a paragraph that is in no list, the typed characters and the space are removed and the paragraph becomes a numbered list in 1, 2, 3 or A, B, C, or a bulleted list with the filled dot. A list started with 1) or A) also writes its numbers with a period. A paragraph whose left and first-line indents are both 0 gets 18 pt and -18 pt. The tool runs text.startList {id, at}, with at the byte after the space, as an undo step of its own after the typing: one Undo puts the characters back as text with no list, and typing goes on after them; Redo removes them again, and typing goes on before the paragraph's text. The same characters later in a paragraph start nothing, and so do list characters that arrive in one piece with the space: a paste, an IME commit, or type_text of a longer string. From a script, text.startList {id, at?} (at defaults to the end of the text) does the same with the text before at and returns started and caret. When the list starts, caret is the byte where the paragraph's text now starts; when the text before at starts no list, nothing changes, started is false and caret is at.

{"name":"run_command","arguments":{"command":"text.setFormat","params":{"bullet":"square"}}}
{"name":"run_command","arguments":{"command":"text.setFormat","params":{"number":"upperRoman"}}}
{"name":"run_command","arguments":{"command":"text.convertListToText","params":{}}}

Small Caps, Superscript and Subscript

text.setFormat {smallCaps, position} (the selected type, or ids) and text.setRangeStyle {id, start, end, smallCaps, position} (a range) set what the Character panel's Small Caps, Superscript and Subscript buttons and menu items set. smallCaps: true draws lowercase letters as capitals at Document Setup's small caps size (70% of the font size by default); capitals keep their size, and with All Caps on every letter is a full-size capital. position: "superscript" or "subscript" draws the characters at Document Setup's superscript or subscript size, raised or lowered by its position (by default 58.3% of the font size, moved by 33.3% of it), and "normal" puts them back on the baseline. A file whose characters, character styles or graph label styles hold a superscript or subscript size outside 1 to 500%, a position outside -500 to 500% or a small caps size outside 1 to 200% opens with the nearer end of each range. Setting superscript on subscript characters replaces the subscript, and setting subscript on superscript characters replaces the superscript. When document.setup {superscript, subscript, smallCapsSize} changes the proportions, the characters take the new ones. One undo step. {"name":"run_command","arguments":{"command":"text.setFormat","params":{"position":"superscript","smallCaps":false}}}

Character Alignment

text.setFormat {charAlign} (the selected type, or ids) and text.setRangeStyle {id, start, end, charAlign} (a range) set where characters smaller than the largest on their line line up with it: romanBaseline (the default), the em box's emBoxTop, emBoxCenter or emBoxBottom, or the ideographic character face's (ICF) icfTop or icfBottom. In vertical type top and bottom are the right and left of the column. The ICF comes from the font's BASE table (icfb, icft, as the OpenType baseline tags define them), else from the average ink box of some ideographs and kana; a font without ideographs uses its em box. One undo step.

{"name":"run_command","arguments":{"command":"text.setFormat","params":{"charAlign":"icfTop"}}}

Proportional Metrics

text.setFormat {proportionalMetrics: true} (the selected type, or ids) and text.setRangeStyle {id, start, end, proportionalMetrics} (a range) set full-width glyphs on the proportional widths the font gives them with the OpenType palt feature; glyphs the font doesn't re-space keep their full width. In vertical type, upright glyphs take the proportional heights of the font's vpal feature (the glyphs are shaped top to bottom for it) and move with them; a font without vertical metrics keeps its cells. Line-end Punctuation Half Width then takes nothing more off the punctuation palt or vpal re-spaced (the opening bracket at a line's start, consecutive punctuation, the closing mark at a line's end); punctuation left full width is trimmed as before. With automatic kerning the font's kerning for the re-spaced glyphs comes too: apkn with palt, and vkrn and vapk with vpal (vertical type is otherwise not kerned). Off by default and saved only when on. One undo step.

{"name":"run_command","arguments":{"command":"text.setFormat","params":{"proportionalMetrics":true}}}

Variable fonts

text.setFormat {fontAxes: {"wght": 650}} (the selected type, or ids) and text.setRangeStyle {id, start, end, fontAxes} (a range) set variation axes of a variable font, as the Character panel's Variable Font sliders do: each tag named takes the value given, in the axis's own units and clamped to its range, in place of the style's setting of that axis, and null sets it back to the style's setting. The other axes keep their settings. A tag the font has no axis for is left out, and a value equal to the style's setting is not kept; for a font that isn't installed the values are kept as given. Layout, outlines, the substituted-glyph highlight and PDF export use the font instanced at the settings; SVG export writes them as font-variation-settings (and the wght setting as the weight, the wdth setting as font-stretch) and embeds the font instanced there, with a warning when embedded faces of one weight, style and width share characters; SVG import reads font-variation-settings back. Another font or style (text.setStyle {font, style}, text.setRangeStyle {font, style}, text.replaceFont, and a character style applied or redefined with another font unless it sets them) clears the settings, and Missing Glyph Protection keeps them on the characters that keep their font. They are saved as fontAxes only when set, and a character style captured from type (charStyle.new, charStyle.redefine) sets them explicitly, {} for none. Each command is one undo step.

{"name":"run_command","arguments":{"command":"text.fontList","params":{"family":"Bahnschrift","style":"Regular"}}}
{"name":"run_command","arguments":{"command":"text.setFormat","params":{"fontAxes":{"wght":650,"wdth":87.5}}}}

Character Direction

text.setFormat {charDirection: "default"|"leftToRight"|"rightToLeft"} (the selected type, or ids) and text.setRangeStyle {id, start, end, charDirection} (a range) set the direction of the characters inside their paragraph, as the Character panel menu's Character Direction does with the Middle Eastern (Indic) options shown. The bidi algorithm (UAX #9) resolves each stretch of characters with a direction as an embedding in that direction, as if an LRE or RLE stood before it and a PDF after it: leftToRight keeps a date written with spaces, such as 21 10 2026, in its order inside a right-to-left paragraph, and rightToLeft sets a Hebrew or Arabic word and the punctuation after it right to left inside a left-to-right paragraph. Characters keep their own strong direction inside an embedding: Latin letters set Right to Left still read left to right. default leaves the characters to the bidi algorithm. The paragraph's own direction is unchanged. SVG export writes type with a character drawn right to left, or with a run set Right to Left, as outlines; live SVG text writes direction="ltr" and unicode-bidi="embed" on each run set Left to Right. Character Direction is Default until set and is saved only when set, and character styles captured from type carry it. Each command is one undo step.

{"name":"run_command","arguments":{"command":"text.setRangeStyle","params":{"id":12,"start":9,"end":19,"charDirection":"leftToRight"}}}

Digits

text.setFormat {digits: "default"|"arabic"|"hindi"|"farsi"} (the selected type, or ids) and text.setRangeStyle {id, start, end, digits} (a range) set the numeral system the characters' digits are drawn in, as the Character panel menu's Digits does with the Middle Eastern (Indic) options shown: arabic draws every digit as the Western 0–9, hindi as the Arabic-Indic ٠–٩ (U+0660–U+0669) and farsi as the Eastern Arabic-Indic ۰–۹ (U+06F0–U+06F9), whichever of the three sets was typed; default leaves each digit as typed. The text keeps the digits that were typed (text.getRange, search, the caret and selection are unchanged); only the glyphs change, as with All Caps, and SVG export writes the digits as drawn. A digit the font lacks comes from a fallback font. Default and saved only when set. One undo step.

{"name":"run_command","arguments":{"command":"text.setFormat","params":{"digits":"hindi"}}}

Touch Type

The Touch Type tool (touchType, Shift+T) changes one character at a time. A click on a character of a type object (pointer_gesture with touchType) selects the object and that character and shows a box around the character. Dragging the box or its lower-left handle moves the character along its line and up or down. The upper-left handle scales it vertically, the lower-right handle scales it horizontally, the upper-right handle scales it proportionally, and the handle in the middle of the top edge rotates it about its pen position on the baseline. Escape lets go of the character. Each drag is one text.touchType {id, start, dx?, dy?, hScale?, vScale?, rotation?} and one undo step. start is the byte offset of the character; a ligature (one glyph for several characters) changes as a whole, and the reply's start and end give its bytes. dx (document points, in reading order) changes the kerning of the character before it on its line and its own kerning, so the character moves by dx and the characters after it stay where they are. The first character of a line and a right-to-left character take no dx, and dx is limited to what kerning from -1000 to 10000 (1/1000 em) on the two characters can give. dy raises it by baseline shift, which moves a rotated character along its own up axis. The tool splits a drag of a character rotated by up to about 84 degrees between kerning and baseline shift, so the character follows the pointer; nearer a quarter turn, a drag moves it only along its line. hScale, vScale and rotation set its scales and rotation as text.setRangeStyle does. Where the font kerned the character with its neighbors, the kerning of the two characters keeps it and the characters after it where they were along the line; a wider or narrower character moves the characters after it by the change in its width. Touch Type gives the character before it a kerning value of its own (with dx, and with any other change where the font kerned the character with its neighbors), and that character can then move by the font's kerning pair with the one before it, as with any style change to one character (text.setRangeStyle). The type stays editable.

{"name":"run_command","arguments":{"command":"text.touchType","params":{"id":7,"start":1,"dx":10,"dy":4}}}

New type in a Japanese interface

While the interface is in Japanese (Preferences › User Interface › Language, or auto on a Japanese system), new type starts with em box top-to-top leading (leadingModel: "emBoxTop") and em box centre character alignment (charAlign: "emBoxCenter"); in other languages it starts on the Roman baseline (#432). This covers the Type tools, text.create and text.createInPath, which also take leadingModel and charAlign to choose, and paraStyle.new / charStyle.new made with no attrs and no text selected. The values used are kept in the journal, so a replay sets the same in any language. Saved documents keep what they say, and imported text (PDF, SVG, DXF…) keeps the Roman baseline.

{"name":"run_command","arguments":{"command":"text.create","params":{"x":40,"y":60,"text":"雅楽","leadingModel":"emBoxTop","charAlign":"emBoxCenter"}}}

Moving and flipping type on a path

Type on a path flows between a start and an end bracket, stored as fractions of its path's length. type.pathOptions {start?, end?, flip?, effect?, alignToPath?, spacing?, ids?} sets them and the rest of Type on a Path Options: end: null puts the end bracket back at the end of the path (or once round a closed one); flip then turns the type to the other side of its path, the path running the other way and the brackets swapping ends so the type keeps its stretch of the path; alignToPath runs the ascender, descender, center or baseline (the default) along the path; spacing (points) closes glyphs up round the outside of curves and opens them up round the inside. With none of them it queries the first selected type on a path: {start, end, flip: false, effect, alignToPath, spacing}. The Options dialog shows Effect, Flip, Align to Path and Spacing and leaves the brackets where they are. As in the reference app, the Selection and Direct Selection tools show selected type on a path's brackets: dragging the start or end bracket (pointer_gesture) sets where the type begins or ends, dragging the centre bracket slides the type along its path and, dragged across the path, flips it (with Cmd/Ctrl held it only slides). Each is one undo step.

{"name":"run_command","arguments":{"command":"type.pathOptions","params":{"start":0.25,"end":0.75,"flip":true}}}

Paragraph attributes per paragraph

Each paragraph (text split at \n) has its own alignment, indents, space before and after, hyphenation, punctuation spacing (mojikumi), hanging punctuation (burasagari), direction, leading model, tab stops and paragraph style. text.setStyle {justify}, text.setFormat {leftIndent, rightIndent, firstLineIndent, spaceBefore, spaceAfter, hyphenate, mojikumi, burasagari, direction, leadingModel}, text.tabs.set / text.tabs.clear and paraStyle.apply take optional start / end byte offsets of the plain text (as text.setRangeStyle does): they change the paragraphs the range touches (a caret, start == end, touches its paragraph). Without a range they change every paragraph of the targeted objects, as before. With a range, the character attributes of text.setStyle and text.setFormat style that range of characters. text.tabs.get {start?} and paraStyle.new / paraStyle.redefine {id, start?} read the paragraph at start; paraStyle.list counts paragraphs as uses. A Return the Type tool types (or text.editRange inserting \n) continues the style of the paragraph it splits; deleting a break keeps the first paragraph's style; text.setText gives every paragraph the first one's attributes. Threaded text keeps each paragraph's attributes as the story re-flows (a paragraph split between frames has them in both).

In the native file a text object's para holds the first paragraph's attributes and paras (left out when every paragraph is alike) one entry per paragraph, so older readers keep the first paragraph's. SVG export anchors each centred or right-aligned line on its own (<tspan text-anchor>), and SVG import gives each line's paragraph its alignment back, with indents that keep it in place. When a native file's type or character styles hold a size, a horizontal scale or a vertical scale of 0 or less, the value opens as 12 pt or 100%. A negative leading opens as Auto, and a size, leading or scale past 4,000,000 opens as 4,000,000. A baseline shift, an indent or a space before or after past 4,000,000 pt either way, in the type or in character or paragraph styles, opens at that limit, and a tab stop outside 0 to 100,000 pt opens at the nearer end.

{"name":"run_command","arguments":{"command":"text.setStyle","params":{"id":42,"justify":"center","start":0,"end":0}}}
{"name":"run_command","arguments":{"command":"text.setFormat","params":{"ids":[42],"spaceBefore":6,"start":12,"end":30}}}
{"name":"run_command","arguments":{"command":"paraStyle.apply","params":{"name":"Heading","id":42,"start":0,"end":0}}}

Constrain proportions

The link between W and H in the Transform panel, the Properties panel and the Control bar is the constrainProportions preference (prefs.get / prefs.set). With it on, those fields send proportional: true to object.setBounds, which scales the other dimension by the same factor; agents pass proportional themselves.

{"name":"run_command","arguments":{"command":"prefs.set","params":{"key":"constrainProportions","value":true}}}
{"name":"run_command","arguments":{"command":"object.setBounds","params":{"width":200,"proportional":true}}}

Transform panel menu

The Transform panel menu's Transform Object Only, Transform Pattern Only and Transform Both are the transformPanelPatterns preference (object, pattern, both). Once one is chosen, the fields, flips and menu flips of the Transform panel and the Properties panel's Transform section send it as objects (false for pattern) and patterns (true for pattern and both) to object.setBounds, object.rotate, object.shear and object.reflect; agents pass those switches themselves. Until then (default, also after prefs.reset) they send neither, so pattern tiles follow transformPatternTiles, and the menu checks Transform Both while it is on and Transform Object Only while it is off.
